Um… I don’t think I’d say I’m worrying excessively, no. I mean, I worry about normal things, like if I forgot to call someone back or if I’m going to look irresponsible because I missed something, but that feels more tied to the actual forgetting.

People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there.   },

    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… when you put it that way, I think it probably goes back a lot further than I usually think about. Like, even in elementary school I was kind of that kid who forgot permission slips or would leave something important crumpled at the bottom of my backpack. I remember losing homework in my backpack, which sounds ridiculous because it was technically with me, but I just wouldn’t find it or remember to turn it in.

And with projects, I usually needed my mom or the teacher to sort of walk me through it step by step. Like, “Okay, do this part now, then put it in your folder, then bring it tomorrow,” because if it was just left up to me, some piece of it would get missed. I don’t know if people saw it as a big problem at the time or just me being scattered, but it wasn’t exactly brand new. It feels like the same kind of thing, just now it shows up with adult responsibilities instead of school papers.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, it’s definitely more than one area. I wouldn’t say my life is falling apart or anything like that—I’m still getting by, I still show up, I still do my job—but it shows up enough that it worries me.

At work, for example, I’m a secretary, so there are all these little follow-ups and details that matter. Like someone asks me to check back on something, or send a reminder, or make sure a form gets to the right place, and I’ll mean to do it, but then if I don’t write it down in exactly the right way or put it somewhere obvious, it can slip. Usually it’s small things, not huge disasters, but it’s happened often enough that I get nervous people will start seeing me as unreliable.

And then outside of work, I’ve had trouble with deadlines too, especially with school-related things when I’ve been trying to handle classes or paperwork. I’ll know something is due, and then somehow it’s like it sneaks up on me or I lose track of the steps to get it done. So it’s not just one place.

The part that bothers me is that I’ve been hoping I could eventually move into more of an Office Manager role, and I worry that these missed follow-ups or missed deadlines could make people think I’m not ready for that. I feel like I can do the work, but the organization part makes me anxious about my reputation.

I mean, I worry about normal things, like if I forgot to call someone back or if I’m going to look irresponsible because I missed something, but that feels more tied to the actual forgetting.\n\nI don’t really feel like I’m sitting around worrying about every part of my life all day, or like I can’t shut my mind off because of worry. It’s more like something happens, I realize I messed up or forgot, and then I feel stressed or annoyed about that specific thing. But more days than not, just general excessive worry? I haven’t really noticed that.\n\nDoctor: Did you have similar difficulties with forgetfulness, disorganization, or losing track of tasks back in childhood or your teenage years, or is this a change that started more recently?\n\nPatient: Yeah… when you put it that way, I think it probably goes back a lot further than I usually think about. Like, even in elementary school I was kind of that kid who forgot permission slips or would leave something important crumpled at the bottom of my backpack. I remember losing homework in my backpack, which sounds ridiculous because it was technically with me, but I just wouldn’t find it or remember to turn it in.\n\nAnd with projects, I usually needed my mom or the teacher to sort of walk me through it step by step. Like, “Okay, do this part now, then put it in your folder, then bring it tomorrow,” because if it was just left up to me, some piece of it would get missed. I don’t know if people saw it as a big problem at the time or just me being scattered, but it wasn’t exactly brand new. It feels like the same kind of thing, just now it shows up with adult responsibilities instead of school papers.\n\nDoctor: Do these forgetfulness and organization problems show up in more than one area of your life—for example, both at work and at home or in your relationships—or is it mostly limited to one setting?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it’s definitely more than one area. I wouldn’t say my life is falling apart or anything like that—I’m still getting by, I still show up, I still do my job—but it shows up enough that it worries me.\n\nAt work, for example, I’m a secretary, so there are all these little follow-ups and details that matter. Like someone asks me to check back on something, or send a reminder, or make sure a form gets to the right place, and I’ll mean to do it, but then if I don’t write it down in exactly the right way or put it somewhere obvious, it can slip. Usually it’s small things, not huge disasters, but it’s happened often enough that I get nervous people will start seeing me as unreliable.\n\nAnd then outside of work, I’ve had trouble with deadlines too, especially with school-related things when I’ve been trying to handle classes or paperwork. I’ll know something is due, and then somehow it’s like it sneaks up on me or I lose track of the steps to get it done. So it’s not just one place.\n\nThe part that bothers me is that I’ve been hoping I could eventually move into more of an Office Manager role, and I worry that these missed follow-ups or missed deadlines could make people think I’m not ready for that. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, that happens to me a lot. I’ll be trying to focus on something at work, like entering information or following up on a message, and then one phone rings or people start talking nearby and suddenly I’m not with the task anymore. It’s like my attention just gets pulled sideways before I even realize it.

And it’s not always something big, either. Sometimes it’s just a random thought, like, “Oh, did I remember to do that thing at home?” and then I’m mentally off in that direction for a minute. Then I come back and have to figure out where I was, which can be frustrating because I really was trying to concentrate.

It happened in class too, when I was taking classes. Someone shifting around, talking quietly, or just some little noise could pull me out of what the instructor was saying. I’d still be sitting there looking like I was listening, but then I’d realize I missed the last few minutes. So yes, I’d say I’m pretty easily distracted, especially if there’s noise or activity around me.

Usually it’s small things, not huge disasters, but it’s happened often enough that I get nervous people will start seeing me as unreliable.\n\nAnd then outside of work, I’ve had trouble with deadlines too, especially with school-related things when I’ve been trying to handle classes or paperwork. I’ll know something is due, and then somehow it’s like it sneaks up on me or I lose track of the steps to get it done. So it’s not just one place.\n\nThe part that bothers me is that I’ve been hoping I could eventually move into more of an Office Manager role, and I worry that these missed follow-ups or missed deadlines could make people think I’m not ready for that. I feel like I can do the work, but the organization part makes me anxious about my reputation.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find your attention drifting or getting easily distracted—like losing track of what you're doing during a task or conversation—even when you're trying to focus?\n\nPatient: Yes, that happens to me a lot. I’ll be trying to focus on something at work, like entering information or following up on a message, and then one phone rings or people start talking nearby and suddenly I’m not with the task anymore. It’s like my attention just gets pulled sideways before I even realize it.\n\nAnd it’s not always something big, either. Sometimes it’s just a random thought, like, “Oh, did I remember to do that thing at home?” and then I’m mentally off in that direction for a minute. Then I come back and have to figure out where I was, which can be frustrating because I really was trying to concentrate.\n\nIt happened in class too, when I was taking classes. Someone shifting around, talking quietly, or just some little noise could pull me out of what the instructor was saying. I’d still be sitting there looking like I was listening, but then I’d realize I missed the last few minutes. So yes, I’d say I’m pretty easily distracted, especially if there’s noise or activity around me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, I do get fidgety, especially if I’m sitting for a long time. Like in longer classes, even when I’m really trying to sit still and pay attention, I’ll notice I’m clicking my pen over and over, or bouncing my foot under the desk, or shifting around in the chair a lot.

It’s not like I’m getting up and pacing around or anything dramatic, but there’s this constant little movement. Sometimes I don’t even realize I’m doing it until I hear the pen clicking or I notice my leg has been bouncing for a while. Then I’ll try to stop, and I can for a bit, but then a few minutes later I’m doing something else—moving in the chair, adjusting how I’m sitting, tapping something.

It’s more noticeable in those situations where I’m supposed to be quiet and still for a long stretch. I can sit there, but it takes effort, if that makes sense.

It feels like the same kind of thing, just now it shows up with adult responsibilities instead of school papers.\n\nDoctor: Do these forgetfulness and organization problems show up in more than one area of your life—for example, both at work and at home or in your relationships—or is it mostly limited to one setting?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it’s definitely more than one area. I wouldn’t say my life is falling apart or anything like that—I’m still getting by, I still show up, I still do my job—but it shows up enough that it worries me.\n\nAt work, for example, I’m a secretary, so there are all these little follow-ups and details that matter. Like someone asks me to check back on something, or send a reminder, or make sure a form gets to the right place, and I’ll mean to do it, but then if I don’t write it down in exactly the right way or put it somewhere obvious, it can slip. Usually it’s small things, not huge disasters, but it’s happened often enough that I get nervous people will start seeing me as unreliable.\n\nAnd then outside of work, I’ve had trouble with deadlines too, especially with school-related things when I’ve been trying to handle classes or paperwork. I’ll know something is due, and then somehow it’s like it sneaks up on me or I lose track of the steps to get it done. So it’s not just one place.\n\nThe part that bothers me is that I’ve been hoping I could eventually move into more of an Office Manager role, and I worry that these missed follow-ups or missed deadlines could make people think I’m not ready for that. I feel like I can do the work, but the organization part makes me anxious about my reputation.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find your attention drifting or getting easily distracted—like losing track of what you're doing during a task or conversation—even when you're trying to focus?\n\nPatient: Yes, that happens to me a lot. I’ll be trying to focus on something at work, like entering information or following up on a message, and then one phone rings or people start talking nearby and suddenly I’m not with the task anymore. It’s like my attention just gets pulled sideways before I even realize it.\n\nAnd it’s not always something big, either. Sometimes it’s just a random thought, like, “Oh, did I remember to do that thing at home?” and then I’m mentally off in that direction for a minute. Then I come back and have to figure out where I was, which can be frustrating because I really was trying to concentrate.\n\nIt happened in class too, when I was taking classes. Someone shifting around, talking quietly, or just some little noise could pull me out of what the instructor was saying. I’d still be sitting there looking like I was listening, but then I’d realize I missed the last few minutes. So yes, I’d say I’m pretty easily distracted, especially if there’s noise or activity around me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless, fidgety, or have trouble sitting still, or find yourself interrupting others or acting impulsively in conversations?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do get fidgety, especially if I’m sitting for a long time. Like in longer classes, even when I’m really trying to sit still and pay attention, I’ll notice I’m clicking my pen over and over, or bouncing my foot under the desk, or shifting around in the chair a lot.\n\nIt’s not like I’m getting up and pacing around or anything dramatic, but there’s this constant little movement. Sometimes I don’t even realize I’m doing it until I hear the pen clicking or I notice my leg has been bouncing for a while. Then I’ll try to stop, and I can for a bit, but then a few minutes later I’m doing something else—moving in the chair, adjusting how I’m sitting, tapping something.\n\nIt’s more noticeable in those situations where I’m supposed to be quiet and still for a long stretch. I’m not really sure that I do that much, honestly. I mean, I can think of times where I get excited or I think I know what someone is about to say, and I might sort of start to respond before they’re completely done, but I don’t know if that’s something that happens often enough that I’d call it a real pattern.

Like returning phone calls, or turning in a registration form, or remembering to stop for an errand on my way home. \n\nIt’s not one big dramatic thing, exactly, but it’s been happening enough that it’s starting to bother me. I’ll have every intention of doing something, and then later I realize I just… didn’t do it. And then I feel embarrassed or frustrated, because these are basic things I’m used to keeping track of.\n\nDoctor: Over the past few weeks, have you noticed feeling down, sad, or less interested in things you normally enjoy?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so, not in a way that really stands out to me. I mean, I get frustrated with myself when I realize I forgot something or missed something I meant to do, and that can kind of put me in a bad mood for a little while. But I wouldn’t say I’ve been feeling sad all the time or like I don’t enjoy things anymore.\n\nI still want to do the things I normally like doing. It’s more that I get annoyed or embarrassed when I drop the ball on regular stuff, if that makes sense. So… no, I haven’t really noticed feeling down or losing interest over the past few weeks.\n\nDoctor: Aside from feeling frustrated about forgetting things, do you find yourself worrying excessively about various aspects of your daily life, more days than not?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think I’d say I’m worrying excessively, no. I mean, I worry about normal things, like if I forgot to call someone back or if I’m going to look irresponsible because I missed something, but that feels more tied to the actual forgetting.\n\nI don’t really feel like I’m sitting around worrying about every part of my life all day, or like I can’t shut my mind off because of worry. It’s more like something happens, I realize I messed up or forgot, and then I feel stressed or annoyed about that specific thing. But more days than not, just general excessive worry? I haven’t really noticed that.\n\nDoctor: Did you have similar difficulties with forgetfulness, disorganization, or losing track of tasks back in childhood or your teenage years, or is this a change that started more recently?\n\nPatient: Yeah… when you put it that way, I think it probably goes back a lot further than I usually think about. Like, even in elementary school I was kind of that kid who forgot permission slips or would leave something important crumpled at the bottom of my backpack. I remember losing homework in my backpack, which sounds ridiculous because it was technically with me, but I just wouldn’t find it or remember to turn it in.\n\nAnd with projects, I usually needed my mom or the teacher to sort of walk me through it step by step. Like, “Okay, do this part now, then put it in your folder, then bring it tomorrow,” because if it was just left up to me, some piece of it would get missed. I don’t know if people saw it as a big problem at the time or just me being scattered, but it wasn’t exactly brand new. It feels like the same kind of thing, just now it shows up with adult responsibilities instead of school papers.\n\nDoctor: Do these forgetfulness and organization problems show up in more than one area of your life—for example, both at work and at home or in your relationships—or is it mostly limited to one setting?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it’s definitely more than one area. I wouldn’t say my life is falling apart or anything like that—I’m still getting by, I still show up, I still do my job—but it shows up enough that it worries me.\n\nAt work, for example, I’m a secretary, so there are all these little follow-ups and details that matter. Like someone asks me to check back on something, or send a reminder, or make sure a form gets to the right place, and I’ll mean to do it, but then if I don’t write it down in exactly the right way or put it somewhere obvious, it can slip. Usually it’s small things, not huge disasters, but it’s happened often enough that I get nervous people will start seeing me as unreliable.\n\nAnd then outside of work, I’ve had trouble with deadlines too, especially with school-related things when I’ve been trying to handle classes or paperwork. I’ll know something is due, and then somehow it’s like it sneaks up on me or I lose track of the steps to get it done. So it’s not just one place.\n\nThe part that bothers me is that I’ve been hoping I could eventually move into more of an Office Manager role, and I worry that these missed follow-ups or missed deadlines could make people think I’m not ready for that. I feel like I can do the work, but the organization part makes me anxious about my reputation.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find your attention drifting or getting easily distracted—like losing track of what you're doing during a task or conversation—even when you're trying to focus?\n\nPatient: Yes, that happens to me a lot. I’ll be trying to focus on something at work, like entering information or following up on a message, and then one phone rings or people start talking nearby and suddenly I’m not with the task anymore. It’s like my attention just gets pulled sideways before I even realize it.\n\nAnd it’s not always something big, either. Sometimes it’s just a random thought, like, “Oh, did I remember to do that thing at home?” and then I’m mentally off in that direction for a minute. Then I come back and have to figure out where I was, which can be frustrating because I really was trying to concentrate.\n\nIt happened in class too, when I was taking classes. Someone shifting around, talking quietly, or just some little noise could pull me out of what the instructor was saying. I’d still be sitting there looking like I was listening, but then I’d realize I missed the last few minutes. So yes, I’d say I’m pretty easily distracted, especially if there’s noise or activity around me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless, fidgety, or have trouble sitting still, or find yourself interrupting others or acting impulsively in conversations?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do get fidgety, especially if I’m sitting for a long time. Like in longer classes, even when I’m really trying to sit still and pay attention, I’ll notice I’m clicking my pen over and over, or bouncing my foot under the desk, or shifting around in the chair a lot.\n\nIt’s not like I’m getting up and pacing around or anything dramatic, but there’s this constant little movement. Sometimes I don’t even realize I’m doing it until I hear the pen clicking or I notice my leg has been bouncing for a while. Then I’ll try to stop, and I can for a bit, but then a few minutes later I’m doing something else—moving in the chair, adjusting how I’m sitting, tapping something.\n\nIt’s more noticeable in those situations where I’m supposed to be quiet and still for a long stretch. I can sit there, but it takes effort, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself interrupting people, finishing their sentences, or blurting out answers before they've finished speaking?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure that I do that much, honestly. I mean, I can think of times where I get excited or I think I know what someone is about to say, and I might sort of start to respond before they’re completely done, but I don’t know if that’s something that happens often enough that I’d call it a real pattern.\n\nFinishing people’s sentences… I don’t think I do that regularly. At least no one has really told me that I’m always cutting them off or anything like that. I’m usually more worried about making sure I understand what they’re asking, especially at work, so I tend to wait because I don’t want to answer wrong.\n\nBlurting out answers before someone is finished—I don’t think so, not in a big way. Maybe occasionally in a normal conversation if I’m nervous or trying to be helpful too quickly, but it’s not one of the main things I’ve noticed about myself. I’d say the fidgeting and staying mentally on track are more noticeable to me than interrupting people.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

Distractibility and hyperactive/restless symptoms (fidgeting, foot-bouncing, pen-clicking) also present, supporting combined presentation. Impulsivity/interrupting symptoms are minimal, but sufficient inattentive and hyperactive criteria are met. Mood and anxiety disorders remain unlikely given absence of pervasive sadness or excessive worry.", "is_final": true}

(Note: In adolescents or adults, may be limited to feeling restless.)\n[S013] Quiet_Activity_Difficulty: Often unable to play or engage in leisure activities quietly.\n[S014] Constant_Activity: Is often 'on the go', acting as if 'driven by a motor'.\n[S015] Excessive_Talking: Often talks excessively.\n[S016] Blurting_Answers: Often blurts out an answer before a question has been completed.\n[S017] Waiting_Turn_Difficulty: Often has difficulty waiting his or her turn.\n[S018] Interrupting_Intruding: Often interrupts or intrudes on others (e.g., butts into conversations, games, or activities).\n[S019] Delusions: Fixed beliefs that do not change easily, even in the face of contradictory evidence. Subtypes: Future Hopelessness, Belief in Impossibility of Improvement, Helpless Resignation, Chronic Pessimism\n[S033] Elevated_Irritable_Mood: A distinct period of abnormally and persistently elevated, expansive, or irritable mood.  Subtypes: Elevated Mood, Irritable Mood, Mood Lability\n[S034] Increased_Activity: Abnormally and persistently increased goal-directed activity or energy.  Subtypes: Increased Energy, Hyperactivity, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S035] Inflated_Self_Esteem: Inflated self-esteem or grandiosity (may range from uncritical self-confidence to delusional proportions).  Subtypes: Inflated Self-Confidence, Overestimation of Abilities, Grandiose Delusions\n[S036] Decreased_Sleep: Decreased need for sleep (e.g., feels rested after only 3 hours of sleep).  Subtypes: Reduced Sleep Duration, Loss of Sleep Need, Indifference to Insomnia\n[S037] Pressured_Speech: More talkative than usual or pressure to keep talking.  Subtypes: Talkativeness, Rapid Speech, Pressured Speech\n[S038] Racing_Thoughts: Flight of ideas or subjective experience that thoughts are racing.  Subtypes: Racing Thoughts, Flooding of Thoughts, Inability to Control Thoughts\n[S039] Manic_Distractibility: Attention is too easily drawn to unimportant or irrelevant external stimuli.  Subtypes: Responsiveness to Extraneous Stimuli, Frequent Task Switching, Inability to Sustain Attention\n[S040] Increased_Goal_Activity: Increase in goal-directed activity (at work, school, socially, or sexually) or purposeless psychomotor agitation.  Subtypes: Occupational Hyperactivity, Social Hyperactivity, Hypersexuality, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S041] Painful_Consequence_Behavior: Excessive involvement in activities that have a high potential for painful consequences.  Subtypes: Unrestrained Buying Sprees, Foolish Business Investments, Sexual Indiscretions, Impulsive Risky Behavior\n[S042] Low_Self_Esteem: A persistent tendency to evaluate oneself as incompetent or worthless; chronically low self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Self-Criticism, Reduced Self-Efficacy, Chronic Sense of Inferiority, Lack of Self-Assurance\n[S043] Restrictive_Intake: Persistent restriction of energy intake relative to requirements, leading to significantly low body weight.  Subtypes: Eating Very Small Amounts, Skipping Meals, Exclusion of Specific Food Groups, Strict Calorie Restriction\n[S044] Low_Weight: A body weight that is significantly below the minimum level of health for age, sex, developmental trajectory, and physical health.  Subtypes: Continuous Weight Loss, Insistence on Low Weight\n[S045] Fear_of_Weight_Gain: Intense fear of gaining weight or of becoming fat, even though at a significantly low weight.  Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety. Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others. Subtypes: Chronic Fatigue, Physical Fatigue, Mental Fatigue, Loss of Energy\n[S030] Worthlessness_Guilt: Feelings of worthlessness or excessive or inappropriate guilt (which may be delusional; not merely self-reproach or guilt about being sick).  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Ruminative Guilt, Excessive or Inappropriate Guilt, Delusional Guilt\n[S031] Diminished_Thinking: Diminished ability to think or concentrate, or indecisiveness.  Subtypes: Reduced Concentration, Indecisiveness, Subjective Memory Complaints, Clouded Thinking\n[S032] Death_Suicide: Recurrent thoughts of death, recurrent suicidal ideation, or a suicide attempt.  Subtypes: Recurrent Thoughts of Death, Suicidal Ideation, Suicidal Impulse, Specific Plan for Suicide, Suicide Attempt\n[S083] Hopelessness: A feeling of despair or having no hope for the future.  Subtypes: Future Hopelessness, Belief in Impossibility of Improvement, Helpless Resignation, Chronic Pessimism\n[S033] Elevated_Irritable_Mood: A distinct period of abnormally and persistently elevated, expansive, or irritable mood.  Subtypes: Elevated Mood, Irritable Mood, Mood Lability\n[S034] Increased_Activity: Abnormally and persistently increased goal-directed activity or energy.  Subtypes: Increased Energy, Hyperactivity, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S035] Inflated_Self_Esteem: Inflated self-esteem or grandiosity (may range from uncritical self-confidence to delusional proportions).  Subtypes: Inflated Self-Confidence, Overestimation of Abilities, Grandiose Delusions\n[S036] Decreased_Sleep: Decreased need for sleep (e.g., feels rested after only 3 hours of sleep).  Subtypes: Reduced Sleep Duration, Loss of Sleep Need, Indifference to Insomnia\n[S037] Pressured_Speech: More talkative than usual or pressure to keep talking.  Subtypes: Talkativeness, Rapid Speech, Pressured Speech\n[S038] Racing_Thoughts: Flight of ideas or subjective experience that thoughts are racing.  Subtypes: Racing Thoughts, Flooding of Thoughts, Inability to Control Thoughts\n[S039] Manic_Distractibility: Attention is too easily drawn to unimportant or irrelevant external stimuli.  Subtypes: Responsiveness to Extraneous Stimuli, Frequent Task Switching, Inability to Sustain Attention\n[S040] Increased_Goal_Activity: Increase in goal-directed activity (at work, school, socially, or sexually) or purposeless psychomotor agitation.  Subtypes: Occupational Hyperactivity, Social Hyperactivity, Hypersexuality, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S041] Painful_Consequence_Behavior: Excessive involvement in activities that have a high potential for painful consequences.  Subtypes: Unrestrained Buying Sprees, Foolish Business Investments, Sexual Indiscretions, Impulsive Risky Behavior\n[S042] Low_Self_Esteem: A persistent tendency to evaluate oneself as incompetent or worthless; chronically low self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Self-Criticism, Reduced Self-Efficacy, Chronic Sense of Inferiority, Lack of Self-Assurance\n[S043] Restrictive_Intake: Persistent restriction of energy intake relative to requirements, leading to significantly low body weight.  Subtypes: Eating Very Small Amounts, Skipping Meals, Exclusion of Specific Food Groups, Strict Calorie Restriction\n[S044] Low_Weight: A body weight that is significantly below the minimum level of health for age, sex, developmental trajectory, and physical health.  Subtypes: Continuous Weight Loss, Insistence on Low Weight\n[S045] Fear_of_Weight_Gain: Intense fear of gaining weight or of becoming fat, even though at a significantly low weight. Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry. Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort. Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others. Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns. Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.  Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event. Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety. Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Active Avoidance, Functional Impairment due to Avoidance, Enduring with Distress, Reliance on Safety Signals\n[S065] Obsessions: Recurrent and persistent thoughts, urges, or images that are experienced as intrusive and unwanted, and attempts to ignore or suppress them.  Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.
